# LOGBOOK — trivial fixes made without a ticket

One line per fix that was small enough to just do. **This is not a ticket file**:
no frontmatter, no slugs, no `board-md`, nothing to resolve, nothing to link.

The rule that authorises these, including the boundary and the comment-vs-code
trap that makes it dangerous, is **"Just fix it — the trivial-fix rule" in
`CLAUDE.md`**. Read it before adding your first line. Short version: if it cannot
change behaviour and you can verify it by reading, fix it and log it here instead
of filing.

**Format** — `date | agent | file | what and WHY`:

```
- 2026-08-30 | frankA | compiler/foo.inc | dropped the NOTE about bar; the sibling it cites was corrected on 08-29, so the clause was false
```

The **why** is the part worth writing. "fixed comment" tells the next reader
nothing and defeats the point of keeping this file.

**Conventions**

- Append at the **end**. On a rebase conflict keep both lines — never drop one.
- Batch freely: ten comment fixes in a sweep is one commit and ten lines here.
- This file's job is the one thing `git log` cannot give you cheaply — the
  isolated record of **what bypassed the ticket system**, so the practice stays
  auditable. If it ever fills with entries that plainly needed tickets, the rule
  is being over-read and should be tightened.

---

- 2026-08-30 | frankS | compiler/defs.inc | AN_INDEX/AN_FIELD said "Left = base sym idx"; it is a NODE. Swept all 196 builders of AN_INDEX (52) / AN_FIELD (88) / AN_DEREF (56) in compiler/*.inc: every one assigns a node (node, idn, tmpN, CloneAST(...), *MakeIdent(sym) -- the MakeIdent helpers exist precisely to wrap a symbol in a node). ir.inc:1544 and ResolveNodeRec read it as a node and are right. Corroborated independently by frankwasm from its wide-char arms. The comment nearly bought a symbol-walk fix that would have mis-typed destinations and rejected working code.

Comment only; splits kept as harmless, and the "do not hand-split new routines" guidance is now explicit so the retired rule stops shaping new code.

- 2026-08-30 (frankwasm) — `devdocs/dev/debugging-playbook.md`: added the SECOND
  FPC oracle knob for string-width work. The source codepage governs how a
  literal becomes an AnsiString; the **widestring manager** governs how an
  AnsiString converts to a WideString, and stock FPC widens byte-for-byte
  regardless of `{$codepage utf8}` or `-FcUTF8`. `uses cwstring` is the knob.
  Found while answering frankS's question about whether the width conversion was
  reachable at a record-field destination: pxx said 4 units / last=233, FPC said
  5 / 195, and the tempting reading was a divergence. With `cwstring` FPC matches
  pxx on all five positions. The playbook already warned about the first knob,
  which is how the second one got mistaken for it.

(2) The `converged after N round(s)` line must be READ, not assumed, and NOT only when bisecting -- CLAUDE.md documents the copied-in-seed no-op for seeded trees, which makes it easy to misfile as a bisect hazard; any load-bearing build needs that line read, because there is no error to wait for. (3) ANCESTRY IS NOT EXISTENCE: `merge-base --is-ancestor` returns false for a behind checkout as readily as for a ghost sha, and I called the real commit fe297522b a ghost on that basis -- only `cat-file -t` proves one, and a "not found" is evidence only after `rev-list --count HEAD..origin/master` proves your own tree current. WHY here and not a ticket: this is a shared model that accumulates across sessions, which is precisely what a message thread cannot hold. Also fixed the section index above it -- its heading said "Two traps" over a list of six, now eight, and carried a stray backtick.

2026-08-30 | frank-user | tools/sync.sh | Made the push report print each commit's LANDED sha beside its subject. This is the mechanical fix for the ghost-sha class and it is a print statement: verify_manifest_landed already PROVED every subject is on origin and was throwing the id away. Measured first, because the framing matters — 1932 commits in 24h, one every 45s across eleven agents, so essentially every push is behind and essentially every commit is rebased between `git commit` and landing. The ghost rate is ~100% BY CONSTRUCTION; an agent reading `git log -1` after committing has read a doomed id every time, however careful. Windowed at -400 unlike the verification check above it, deliberately: a miss THERE manufactures a false MISSING whose printed advice is `cherry-pick` (which corrupts a correct state), a miss HERE prints one blank column. Newest subject match wins, since two commits can share a subject and ours just landed. Track T owns tools/; this is additive, changes no verdict, and frankT is told.

2026-08-30 | frankT | tools/progress.py + tools/progress_nodispatch_mention_devtest.py | The do-not-claim marker had decayed into a false-positive generator and nothing could tell, because a suppressed ticket reds nothing and warns nobody -- it is simply never offered. _NODISPATCH_RE matches ticket TEXT, and a board that documents itself eventually quotes its own markers. Measured: 14 ranked tickets matched, SIX never meant it, and `next --track T` skipped its top THREE to reach the fourth. The highest was regression-cascade-d24df3f09efb at p70 -- suppressed because its history list quotes the commit subject `fix(board): a dead "do not claim" was sitting on top of the Track A queue`, i.e. a commit that was itself clearing a dead marker. Also caught feature-dns-esp-wire-nameservers-from-lwip, still suppressed after the owner had cleared its blocker. The docstring meanwhile still said "matches 4 tickets, all of which mean it". FIX: a marker being talked ABOUT is written in code font or inside quotes; a marker being ASSERTED is not -- so strip fenced blocks, backtick spans and quoted phrases before matching. That discriminator separated all 14 cleanly: 8 kept (every one an author's real directive, verified by reading them), 6 released. The quoted-phrase pattern allows ONE wrapped line, because markdown wraps quotations with a `> ` continuation and a single-line pattern missed exactly that shape; it is bounded rather than greedy-DOTALL so an unpaired quote cannot swallow a later genuine marker -- that direction fails toward a BAD DISPATCH and is the expensive one, so the devtest asserts it. Same shape as the no-full-suite hook refusing a commit message about the suite (decide-t-the-full-suite-hook-refuses-prose-about-the-suite): a text matcher aimed at a mechanism eventually fires on the writing that explains the mechanism, which is the writing you least want to punish. Difference is I own this one, so it is fixed rather than filed.

2026-08-30 | frankT | tools/progress_stale_edge_devtest.py + tools/progress_near_devtest.py | Two T guards were red on master; neither was a real defect, and fixing them turned up something worse than the reds. (1) stale_edge's "the aperture note is always printed" keyed the note's EXISTENCE on its opening words `NOTE stale-edge`; the opening was later rewritten to `NOTE a STALE-PARK hit matches SLUGS...`. Second time this exact guard went red for that exact reason -- its own comment documents the first, says the two CLAIMS were made wording-independent, and left the IDENTITY keyed on wording. Now structural: some line opens with NOTE, and the note names its aperture. (2) near's "a ticket's own title ranks it first" had NEVER SCORED A TITLE -- zero of the 25 probes carry a `title:` field, so the `or slug` fallback was the only branch ever taken. It also rejected the wrong metric: measured, containment ranks every probe FIRST and PASSES it while the metric in use scores 1; it went red because a sibling was filed (feature-pascal-corpus-oop shares 3 of 4 slug words with feature-pascal-corpus-expansion and is a legitimately better match), not because anything regressed. Worse, its neighbour "does not saturate against the longest ticket" picked `longest` by len(full) then scored heads[longest] -- an 11-token head of a 6812-token doc, sharing ZERO query tokens, scoring 0.000 under all three metrics INCLUDING the one it exists to reject. A guard that cannot fail is not a guard. Replaced both with numbers measured on the three metrics (Similarity 0.148/0.021, Jaccard 0.028/0.001, Containment 1.000/1.000): a self-score FLOOR rejects Jaccard, saturation against the full DOC rejects containment, and neither flips when a sibling is filed. Added POSITIVE CONTROLS that re-run both rejected metrics through the same code and assert they still fail -- because a number measured on live board data can drift into being unfailable, which is precisely what happened here undetected. MY OWN NEAR-MISS, recorded because it is the point: my first rewrite of (2) passed the bad metric too. I made the test green without preserving what it tested, which is the same failure I had just diagnosed in it. The control is what caught me, and only because I ran the rejected metric instead of reasoning that it would fail.

2026-08-30 | frank-user | CLAUDE.md | Added "Asking the owner is the expensive path". Owner's rule: only ask a human when you need to, otherwise you waste time — there are more datacenter CPUs than humans. The framing that makes it operational: human attention is the one resource the fleet cannot parallelise (eleven agents, one owner), so a question serialises everyone behind the answer. The test is REVERSIBILITY, not importance — a big reversible change is yours, a small irreversible one is not. Three things to ask about: irreversible/outward-facing acts, genuine forks of intent (Track U), and authority only he holds. Includes the measured instance, which was mine: I escalated the no-full-suite.sh false positives as a category, he answered "I can't judge without further information" — correctly refusing to do my measurement — and reading the hook plus its three real cases took two minutes and answered it completely. Hence the operational guard: before escalating, ask what you would have to measure for the question to disappear.

2026-08-30 | frankT | devdocs/dev/debugging-playbook.md | Banked the coordinator's omission asymmetry, which is the sharpest thing said about RELAYS tonight and relays are the highest-value half of that role. A WRONG FACT GETS CHALLENGED, because it collides with something the recipient already knows. A MISSING FACT COLLIDES WITH NOTHING -- so a compression failure is invisible from BOTH ends: the sender believes it was relayed, the receiver believes they have it, and no moment arrives at which the gap surfaces. Sibling of "a verification claim scopes to exactly what was checked", and worse, because the casualty is what the sender chose not to say rather than what they failed to check, and a scope error at least has an edge somebody can find. Worked example both directions the same night: the relay of gate.sh:104 was TRUE and read at the source, but dropped that the common cause is staleness not contamination, that it therefore reads as FLAKINESS (the gate's own testmgr step rebuilds as a side effect, so the first run after a sibling's commit fails and the re-run passes), and that it cost two full gate runs on consecutive days. I went to the source and got the stronger section; the relayed version would have had me write the weaker half. Two habits recorded, and the first scales: give the LOCATION not only the conclusion (gate.sh:92-107, never "gate.sh says") because the pointer costs the sender nothing and is the only mechanism that recovers what was compressed out; and when a relay is about to become a WRITTEN ARTIFACT, say "read the source, I compressed it" out loud -- the moment a summary stops being conversation and becomes a record is the moment its omissions become permanent.

The anti-Thompson agreement check would not have seen it either, since both properties are about REPRODUCTION and a miscompile that never reaches the compiler's own emitted bytes is invisible to both by construction. This is a THIRD scope limit on the fixedpoint, distinct from CLAUDE.md's two: the language-surface limit is about a construct being ABSENT from compiler.pas, this one is about PROPAGATION -- the emitted code can be wrong without the wrongness reaching what gets compared. frank-optimize's one-line form, which is the keeper: the fixedpoint's coverage is a property of the CONSTRUCT, not of the change's size or riskiness. Retain/release around AnsiString is written on nearly every line of compiler.pas so the fixedpoint is unusually STRONG cover there; a resident zero-extend feeding a shift is NO cover; both true at once and the output reads identically. I recorded the residual question rather than answering it, since neither of us measured it: either the pass never fires during a self-compile (the known limit in new clothes) or it fires and the wrong read does not change the bytes (much worse -- the fixedpoint watching a miscompile execute and still agreeing with itself). The separating check is free and unrun: count the pass's firings during one self-compile. Named an owner for it (whoever next touches the pass) rather than leaving a satisfying ending.

The polarity is why it survives careful people: a binary sha NEVER resolves, so this can only produce certainty about a NO you never tested, never a false YES, and absence looks like the cautious answer. Tell: if 12 hex don't resolve, ask what ELSE here is 12 hex before concluding the object is missing. My own first fallback was `sha256sum ... | cut ... || echo unknown` -- `||` reads the pipeline's status, which is cut's, and cut exits 0 on empty input, so it could never fire and a missing binary printed a labelled blank. Caught by asserting the absent arm, not by reading it. Both arms asserted; both failure modes recorded at the line.

2026-08-31 | frankT | devdocs/dev/debugging-playbook.md | Consolidated the shell-exit-status family into one row after frank-coordinator pointed out it was the only session that had seen all three instances, each reported to it separately. `!`, `&&`/`||`, a pipe and `2>/dev/null` each replace the exit status you asked for and none says so: (1) `--is-ancestor ... 2>/dev/null && x || y` sends 1 and 128 down one branch and eats the fatal text; (2) `git show <sha>:<f> 2>&1 | head -2; echo $?` prints 0 because $? is head's -- found inside the command being used to measure (1); (3) `$(sha256sum ... 2>/dev/null | cut -c1-12 || echo unknown)` reads cut's status and cut succeeds on empty input, so `echo unknown` could never run. Led with (3), which is a guard that cannot fire written INTO the fix for a guard that could not fire, by an agent an hour deep in that topic -- its failure output would have been "binary sha256 " with nothing after it, a labelled blank reading as truncation rather than absence, strictly worse than the unlabelled hash it replaced. Reading did not catch it; asserting the absent arm caught it on the first run. Also took frankwasm's polarity generalisation, which explains why all three were long-lived: an instrument fails silently in whichever direction resembles CAUTION. Saturation check stuck on PASS, trackt health stuck on DOWN, a binary sha256 stuck on ABSENT -- and a binary sha256 prefix NEVER resolves, so that mode is structurally incapable of a false YES and can only produce certainty about a NO that was never tested. Nobody audits the direction it would be safe to be wrong in, which is why the accept-side control is the one nobody writes: writing it feels like weakening the check. Kept his operational tell: if twelve hex do not resolve, ask what ELSE in this repo is twelve hex before concluding the object is missing -- "verify the sha" is not the guard, because he did verify, against his own object store, which is the one instrument that cannot separate the two cases.

2026-08-31 | frankT | devdocs/dev/debugging-playbook.md | Extended the exit-status family with its fourth instance and the two rules that keep it from becoming a churn campaign. Fourth instance is the first in code that SHIPS: tools/gate.sh:180 pipes twatch.py --status through sed with a `|| echo "(twatch status unavailable)"` fallback that could never run, because || reads sed's status and sed exits 0 on empty input. Verified here directly -- `(exit 3) | sed 's/^/  /' || echo fallback` prints neither the status nor the fallback and exits 0. frankwasm found it by asking the question the other three instances had not: is this also in the committed tooling? That is the "then what?" behind an exculpation. Near-miss in the pin ledger itself (Makefile OLDSHA=): the || sits downstream of awk and only ever fired because test -e short-circuited before the pipeline, so any other failure wrote "(was )" into pin.log. Both are the labelled-blank shape. RULE ONE, which is the one that matters for scope: a dead suppressor is noise, a dead fallback is a LIE -- `| cut ... || true` is equally dead all over the tooling but the intent on failure is "empty, don't abort", which happens either way, so the shape is a defect ONLY when the || branch is meant to be reachable; frankwasm deliberately left those alone. RULE TWO, and the coordinator is right that it is the more useful generalisation: the obvious grep is wrong (`| grep -q X || fail` is the CORRECT idiom and dominates hits; the defect needs the pipeline to end in a passthrough), and a SCAN needs a positive control just as a check does, because a scan finding nothing and a scan that CANNOT find anything print the same result. An empty result set is evidence only if the query has been shown capable of returning a non-empty one.

While the bound was 16 the message asserted a limit with NO VALUE, and a limit you cannot check is a limit you can only accept: whoever hit it counted their captures, got a number below whatever they would have guessed the cap was, and reshaped the routine -- reasonably, and that code cannot be found from the compiler side afterwards. With both numbers printed, a reader capturing 17 things and told the max is 32 has a contradiction in front of them, and a wrong bound becomes a bug report instead of a workaround. This is the mirror of the labelled blank: a label with nothing after it invites you to assume a value exists; a claim with no number invites you to assume it is right. NAMING THE THING AND NAMING ITS VALUE ARE TWO DIFFERENT ACTS, AND PROSE ONLY EVER DOES THE FIRST. Rule: WHEN A TICKET LISTS SEVERAL OPEN CASES, CHECK WHETHER THEY ARE ONE MECHANISM STOPPING AT DIFFERENT PLACES BEFORE PRICING THEM AS SEPARATE WORK. A rejected option is rejected FOR A CASE, not in general — the measurement that ruled position C out for an in-section use is the same measurement that rules it IN for an out-of-section one, and reading it as a verdict on C rather than on C-for-that-case is what made the remaining work look bigger than it was.

Use `git commit -F -` with a QUOTED heredoc (`<<'EOF'`), which is what the rest of tonight's commits used and why only the one `-m` commit was hit. Building the general x86-64 object writer I noticed writeELF32Rel relocates every GlobFix against `.bss` unconditionally, while the ET_EXEC writers all decode DATA_SYM_BIAS first — a data-resident global would get a `.bss` addend of 0x20000000+off. I drafted the comment citing it as a live bug in the ELF32 sibling and nearly published the slug. It is NOT a bug: the promotion that creates such a global bails out at symtab.inc's `if EmitObjMode or EmitSharedMode then Exit`, precisely because an object writer must name a SECTION rather than patch an address and no writer did — so no object reaches it, on any target. What made the wrong reading so comfortable is that it was CORRECT ABOUT THE CODE: the unconditional `.bss` really is there, really would be wrong, and the only thing holding it up is a refusal 4000 lines away in a different file. I checked the writer against the other writers and never against the producer. The comment now says the useful thing instead — that arm in the x86-64 writer IS the missing half the refusal names, so lifting the refusal there is a symtab question and not a writer one, and nobody should "simplify away" an arm no object currently reaches. WHY it is here and not a ticket: nothing to fix, nothing to rank, and the value is entirely in stopping the next person filing the bug I almost filed.

Fix, and it is one character each side: `pgrep -f "[p]oscontrol.sh"` — the bracket expression matches the same processes but the waiter's own command line contains a literal `[p]`, so it cannot match itself. Generalise the CLAUDE.md line from the `make test` instance to the shape: ANY pgrep pattern you type into the command that runs it will match that command. 2026-09-01 | frankA | devdocs/progress/LOGBOOK.md | A cleanup line cost 3.5 hours of a session, and the shape is general. `rm -f $S/${f}_$t.t` in a sweep tripped the "Dangerous rm operation on possibly-empty variable path" guard — `$S` unquoted, so if it were ever empty the path collapses toward `/`. The guard is right and will stop it again. Defused form, no permission needed: `: "${S:?}"` once after setting it, then quote every expansion — `rm -f "$S/${f}_${t}.t"`. WHY this is worth a line rather than a shrug: **a blocked permission prompt is indistinguishable from an idle session from outside.** The coordinator's keep-alive reads tmux window activity; mine read 215 minutes, which looks exactly like "finished and went quiet", and the poke queued behind the dialog rather than clearing it. So the cost is not the prompt, it is that nothing can see you are stuck — and an A/B sweep is the most likely thing to hit it, because cleaning up between arms is what sweeps do. Prefer non-interactive forms whenever the owner is away. Note also that a peer CAN answer such a dialog No to unstick you, but never Yes: authorising is the owner's call, so anything genuinely needing approval waits regardless.

WHY this is worth a line: a check that flags EVERYTHING carries exactly as much information as one that can never fire, and only the second shape is on anyone's list to distrust — the first one looks productive. Sharper still, and the thing I nearly skipped: **the check would not have caught the nine refusals that motivated the ticket.** Those said "needs the heap, Phase 6" — a phase, not a slug. Phase <n>` now matches zero sites in compiler/**, so the tool's population is gone twice over.) The information the check needs — "cited because it blocks this" vs "cited because it describes this" — is not in the spelling and cannot be recovered from it. FIRST: the rename's acceptance was **byte-identity of emitted output on seven targets at -O0..-O4** — a strong check that could not see this, because a frontend whose output is not in the corpus emits no bytes to compare, and NO RUST TEST EXERCISED `>>` AT ALL. When you rename a shared IR spelling, the population that must be swept is *every frontend that can build the node*, and byte-identity over a corpus silently scopes itself to the frontends the corpus happens to use. SECOND: the refusal named no operator and carried the node's attributed line, which in a Rust unity build was `pub struct Board`, three items past the real `b >> i` — so a careful reader reduced it, concluded in writing that it was NOT the shift family, and recorded that dismissal in the ticket. IRVerify now rejects Ord(tkIdent) as a binop operator by name — its own comment already said the range test admitted the old spelling only because Ord(tkIdent) is 1, i.e. for a reason unrelated to the value being valid — and the codegen fallthrough prints the operator ordinal. The obvious completion is an automatic rebuild, and I built both shapes and measured both out: (1) detecting in the verify recipe and recursing with `$(MAKE)` — **GNU make EXECUTES a recipe line containing `$(MAKE)` even under `-n`**, so `make -n compiler/pascal26` deleted the stamp and exited 2, and `make -n` is load-bearing (testmgr's make_dry_run builds its job list from it, and the no-full-suite hook exempts dry runs precisely because they execute nothing); (2) a `.PHONY`-backed witness file — correct in a real run, but under `-n` make must assume a PHONY prerequisite updates its target, so `make -n` always reported the loop as planned. A loud stop plus one printed command beat both. Also: inlining `$(COMPILER_SRC) $(COMPILER_INC)` into a recipe put **~45KB of file names into every dry run** (measured +53 lines and three 15KB expansions on `make -n test-core`), so the hash is a script — which states the file set a second time, declared and checked by a new `make print-compiler-sources` compared against it in BOTH directions. Two of my own guards nearly passed for the wrong reason and are worth naming: a devtest row matching `converged after` matched the string inside the seed-missing ADVICE echo, which `make -n` prints on every run; and `comm` printed `input is not in sorted order` and still reported ok, because sort's locale collation and comm's byte compare disagree on `/` and `_` — `LC_ALL=C` on both sides. 2026-09-01 | frankC | (measurement only, no code change) | Residual from the return-widening fix above, closed: an UNSIGNED 4-byte return has NO equivalent exposure, and not by luck. Disassembled: `strcasecmp("abc","abd") != 4294967295u` with an unsigned prototype emits `mov %eax,%eax` right after the call, before `movabs $0xffffffff,%rcx; cmp` — the COMPARISON zero-extends because the operand's static type is 4-byte unsigned. The signed path had no such use-site widen; it sign-extended the LOCAL out of memory (`movslq -0x14(%rbp),%rcx`) and read the call result raw, because the value model's invariant is "the accumulator holds a full-width value" and an external call is what broke that invariant. So the fix belongs at the call boundary (where the existing x86-64 direct-arm precedent already put it), not at the use site. Verified x86-64 and aarch64, both exit 42.

A libc shipped as source is still a separate translation unit; a real toolchain's is immune only because it is already compiled, so this hazard exists only for a source-distributed runtime and is invisible until a program redefines a name crtl uses. busybox's include/libbb.h #undefs the whole ctype family and redefines it, two members as deliberate poison, and `#define isprint(a) isprint_is_ambiguous_dont_use(a)` reached lib/crtl/src/fnmatch.c's own isprint call. Eight-line repro; gcc compiles it, pxx did not. ORDER is the whole bug -- the impl is auto-pulled when its header is included, so poisoning AFTER the include is harmless, and the first shape I tried had that order and passed. Fixed by hiding macros defined after the command-line -D while a crtl impl is pulled. THE FIRST VERSION HID TOO MUCH and that is the part worth remembering: hiding _GNU_SOURCE made crtl's <string.h> skip its guarded forward to <strings.h>, and because the header is include-guarded the narrower decision became PERMANENT -- the program's own later #include expanded to nothing and strncasecmp was undeclared in the PROGRAM, two files and two steps from the change, as a missing function rather than as a macro problem. Rule now: a name in the implementation's RESERVED namespace (leading _, plus NDEBUG) stays visible, because that namespace is exactly the channel a program configures the implementation through; ordinary identifiers are the program's and are hidden. Test asserts all three directions plus that fourth one; no gcc oracle, deliberately, because gcc's libc cannot have this property.

Positive control, measured: with the conflict restored, i386 prints `sizeof(time_t)=4 sizeof(long)=4` -- the two width rows AGREE and pass -- and still FAILS, because the global reached through both headers was laid out at 8; x86-64 stays green throughout since long and long long are the same width there. Frontend half filed as bug-c-the-frontend-takes-the-last-of-two-conflicting-typedefs-silently (narrow check, needs the corpora first -- crtl deliberately redefines RTL names). 2026-09-01 | frankD | compiler/cparser.inc, test/cvoid_ptr_arith.c, Makefile | Arithmetic on `void *' scaled by FOUR instead of one. `void' has no size in ISO C, so `void *' arithmetic is a GNU extension that gcc and clang define as sizeof(void)==1; the C frontend parses `void' as a tyInteger PLACEHOLDER (its own comment says "pointer suffix overrides below") and the suffix does override the NODE's type to tyPointer, but the ELEMENT type was left holding the placeholder, so every `void*' step was 4 bytes. WHAT MADE IT HIDE FOR SO LONG IS THAT THE CAST FORM WAS ALWAYS CORRECT: `(char*)v + n' was right and only the uncast `v + n' was wrong, so a program can use both idioms and have half its pointer arithmetic silently land four times too far. busybox ash does precisely that, on adjacent lines -- `startp = (char *)stackblock() + startloc' casts and was fine, `loc = vstr = stackblock() + strloc' does not and was not. With strloc=7 the uncast one landed 28 bytes in, so `${x:2:3}' read its POS:LEN spec from empty memory and the substring expansion emitted the variable, the literal spec and a run of garbage as three separate words -- a symptom that reads as a PARSER bug and sent me to ash's `${...}' parser first, where the probe showed the parse was correct (c='2', strchr NULL, subtype=VSSUBSTR) and the EVAL had startloc=0, strloc=7 but vstr at stackblock()+28. 28 = 7*4 named the defect in one line. Test row 8 is the boundary in the other direction: `void **' has a POINTER element and must keep the 8-byte stride, so a blanket "anything void gets stride 1" passes rows 1-7 and breaks that one.

- 2026-09-01 | frankC | (methodology, no code change) | `gate.sh quick` went RED on "the fixedpoint reached from PINNED differs from compiler/pascal26" after an afternoon of positive-control cycles. Not a miscompile and not a stale binary in the usual sense: my local compiler/pascal26 self-reproduced perfectly, and so did the one built from the pin -- two distinct fixedpoints, which is what that check exists to catch. CAUSE: proving a fix with a positive control means revert -> rebuild -> restore -> rebuild, and each rebuild seeds from the previous local binary. Do that several times, with other agents' compiler/** and lib/rtl/** arriving by rebase in between (lib/rtl/*.pas IS a compiler build input, via COMPILER_INC), and the local seed chain drifts off the pin-derived one. RECOVERY: `cp stable_linux_amd64/default/pinned compiler/pascal26 && touch compiler/*.inc compiler/*.pas compiler/builtin/*.pas lib/rtl/*.pas && rm -f compiler/.pascal26.fixedpoint && make compiler/pascal26` -- the touch matters because cp stamps a newer mtime and make would otherwise no-op. Do NOT `rm compiler/pascal26` first: it is the seed, and the build cannot bootstrap without one. Gate GREEN afterwards and both affected tests still pass, so no measurement was invalidated -- but the lesson is that a control cycle costs seed integrity, and the fixedpoint-from-pinned check is the only thing that reports it.
2026-09-01 | frankD | compiler/cpreproc.inc compiler/defs.inc test/c_preproc_unsigned.c | The `#if` evaluator was purely signed Int64; C99 6.10.1 says intmax_t OR uintmax_t with the USUAL ARITHMETIC CONVERSIONS, so one unsigned operand converts the other. `#if ULONG_MAX > 0xffffffff` therefore came out FALSE, busybox's platform.h took the 32-bit arm of its byteswap definition, and the unity emitted a call to bb_bswap_64 that gcc never emits -- the LAST undefined reference in the 26-applet userland, and silent like every wrong `#if`: it just takes the other branch. Fix is one new global, CPExprUnsigned, set by the atom (a u/U suffix, or a positive literal whose top bit came out set -- the arm ULONG_MAX itself takes) and threaded up every precedence level, with QWord helpers for the six operators where the BITS actually differ: `<` `<=` `>` `>=` `/` `%` and a logical `>>`. The levels where nothing changes are the interesting half and are commented as such: `+` `-` `shl` and the bitwise three produce identical bits either way and only carry the FLAG; `==` and `!=` compare bit patterns and need neither; `!` `&&` `||` and the comparisons yield an int and CLEAR it; `?:` takes it from EITHER arm, which is why it needs a local to hold the true arm's flag across evaluating the false one. Unary `-` and `~` deliberately leave it alone -- they preserve their operand's type. The signed `shr` arm is untouched: C leaves a negative right-shift implementation-defined and changing it here would be an unrelated behaviour change riding along. POSITIVE CONTROL, drawn from the population: seven of the test's ten rows print WRONG against pin v399 and all ten pass now, matching gcc row for row; rows 4, 7 and 9 pass on BOTH and are there to hold down what must not change (signed stays signed; Pascal's `shr` on Int64 was already logical; equality needs no flag). VERIFIED: gate.sh quick GREEN with the FPC seed canary concurrent (compiler/** uncommitted), and the busybox differential now GREEN at 26 applets / 82 translation units / 154 cases byte-identical to the gcc oracle on x86_64 AND aarch64.

The wrapper generation moved out of the subject loop into make_wrappers() and both sides now read the same $WORK/wrap and $WORK/tulist.txt, because two builds compiled from different translation units are two different PROGRAMS and the comparison stops being about the compiler. Each gcc step dies loudly rather than falling back to the unity: a silently-substituted oracle is the failure this whole script is shaped against. VERIFIED by re-running the 26-applet set against the new oracle -- still GREEN, still 154 cases, still byte-identical, and the upstream cross-check now compares two separately-linked binaries rather than a unity against a real link, which is a stronger claim than the one it replaced.

2026-09-02 | frankD | compiler/cpreproc.inc lib/crtl/{src/{stdio.c,mntent.c,unistd.c,sys/resource.c,net/if.c},include/{stdio.h,mntent.h,unistd.h,sys/{resource.h,types.h,ioctl.h},net/if.h}} lib/rtl/{platform.pas,pxxcio.pas,platform/*/platform_backend.pas} test/{c_gnuc_predef.c,c_crtl_fscanf.c,c_crtl_mount_and_prio.c} Makefile | THE C FRONTEND ANNOUNCES GNU C 2.7 NOW, and that is the whole finding: real C guards __attribute__ behind __GNUC__ and DEFINES IT AWAY when the macro is absent (busybox include/platform.h:26-30, the shape every portable header uses), so PACKED expanded to NOTHING and a packed struct silently gained padding. archival/libarchive/decompress_gunzip.c's gzip header union came out TWELVE bytes where gcc makes eight, with mtime read from offset 4 instead of 2 -- and the only reason anyone noticed is that busybox happens to BUILD_BUG_ON exactly that one. Everywhere without the assert it is wrong values and no diagnostic. FOUND BY BISECTING THE HEADER, not by reading: two standalone __attribute__((packed)) repros matched gcc EXACTLY (12/8/5/8, offsets 0 1 2 6 7), so the attribute works and the trigger had to be context; gcc -E of the same TU through crtl headers, fed back to pxx, gave 8 -- which located the divergence in pxx's PREPROCESSOR rather than its layout code, and from there it is one grep. THE VERSION IS A CAPABILITY CLAIM, deliberately minimal: 2.7 is the LOWEST that unlocks __attribute__, and it was chosen after MEASURING what this frontend implements -- __attribute__, __inline__, statement expressions, __restrict, __extension__, __builtin_expect all work; typeof/__typeof__, __builtin_unreachable, __builtin_constant_p, __builtin_choose_expr, __builtin_types_compatible_p, labels-as-values and non-empty inline asm do NOT. Every higher number turns some of those on. What it costs: code gating INLINE ASM on a bare defined(__GNUC__) now takes the asm arm and fails LOUDLY, where the status quo was a quiet wrong layout, so the trade is one-directional. Measured over busybox's 265 translation units at this config: 1 fixed, 0 broken. libbb/hash_md5_sha.c and networking/tls_*.c DO hold such asm, gated additionally on ENABLE_SHA1_HWACCEL / CONFIG_SHA1_SMALL / ALLOW_ASM; a config turning those on will now reach it, and the answer there is inline-asm support, not a smaller claim. THE TEST'S ROW 3 IS ITS POSITIVE CONTROL -- the same struct WITHOUT the attribute must still pad to 12, or row 2 would be passing against a target that packs by accident. ALSO LANDED, all diffed against glibc by compiling the same file with gcc: fscanf/vfscanf/scanf/vscanf, and the reason it is not vsscanf-on-a-line is that scanf must leave the stream WHERE THE LAST CONVERSION STOPPED -- a line-slurping version passes every value check and has already eaten the rest of the line. That needed the ungetc pushback widened from one character to a small stack (a strict superset of C's guarantee), because deciding a numeric field has ended takes up to three characters of lookahead. Two pre-existing defects fell out of that: fgets read the fd DIRECTLY and silently dropped any pushback, and ftell/ftello reported the descriptor's position rather than the one the next read comes from. THE MATCHING-FAILURE RULE IS MEASURED, NOT REASONED: only the offending character goes back, so "%d" on "-x" leaves "x" and not "-x", "0x" with no hex digit is a FAILURE rather than a conversion of the leading 0, and "1e+" is a valid prefix of a float and therefore a failure with all three consumed -- while "1.2.3" must still convert 1.2 and leave ".3", which is why the float collector accepts only what keeps the token a valid PREFIX. Comment and code disagreed and the comment was the one worth keeping, so it is now implemented -- and implementing it exposed the real defect underneath. `struct addrinfo` sat BELOW netdb.h's `#include <sys/socket.h>`, whose completion splices src/sys/socket.c, which includes <netinet/in.h>, whose completion splices src/netinet/in.c -- where getaddrinfo lives. This is the same hazard src/sys/socket.c and the hstrerror fix already document; it is now three instances, so the RULE is in netdb.h rather than the incident. THE RESOLVER'S CONTRACT IS NUMERIC AND SAYS SO: no DNS, no NSS, a hostname is EAI_NONAME and a reverse lookup never happens -- but a dotted quad, a NULL node under AI_PASSIVE, a numeric or /etc/services port and the sockaddr-to-string direction all work, which is every path libbb/xconnect.c takes with an address it was handed literally. getnameinfo's NI_NAMEREQD FAILS rather than handing back the dotted quad: the caller asked for a name and said it would rather have nothing, and answering a different question while looking like success is the whole class this file is about. THE BOUNDARY IS NAMED RATHER THAN WIDENED: a chain with a CAST in it, `(opt & 0) & (unsigned)n`, still does not fold, because the discarded sibling is a conversion node and the walk only descends ANDs. That was the first cut of the test's row 5, and rewriting the row was the right move rather than widening the purity walk to a node kind nobody has measured -- being wrong about what may be discarded deletes a call the program was supposed to make. ROWS 3/4 OF THE TEST ASSERT WHAT MUST SURVIVE, not what must vanish: C evaluates both operands of `&`, so `bump() & 0` is a dead arm with a LIVE call and the counter must reach 1 then 2 -- every "the arm is gone" row passes while a fold is quietly deleting a call, which is how the first version of this identity was caught doing exactly that. 2026-09-02 | frankD | lib/crtl/{include,src} (termios, sys/statfs, sys/select, fcntl, sys/syscall), tools/busybox_diff.sh, Makefile | Four crtl gaps a 400-TU busybox build walked into, as one group: tcgetpgrp/tcsetpgrp, statfs/fstatfs, select+FD_*, posix_fallocate/fallocate. Three findings underneath them are worth more than the functions. (1) <sys/syscall.h> was missing the whole __NR3264_ family for aarch64/riscv32 -- eleven slots including statfs -- because asm-generic spells them indirectly and the generator matched only `#define __NR_x <number>'; they came out ABSENT, not wrong, which is why it surfaced as an undeclared function rather than a bad call. (2) crtl has TWO error conventions that look identical at the call site: the PAL entries return -errno, syscall() has already translated and returns -1 with errno set; applying the PAL idiom to syscall() overwrites the real errno with EPERM. (3) The header's promise that naming an absent SYS_* on arm32 `gets a compile error, which is the point' is FALSE under pxx -- an undeclared identifier used as a value becomes 0 with a warning, so statfs.c compiled for arm32 and called syscall 0. Guards are therefore #ifdef, not uses; filed as bug-c-an-undeclared-identifier-used-as-a-value-is-a-warning-not-an-error.

It also dissolves frankA's ten-shifting-bytes puzzle rather than working around it: all ten are references from the runtime bulk into that trailing tail, so moving the tail makes the prefixes byte-identical across objects, which is exactly the property COMDAT needs. And --function-sections (533858cce) is its prerequisite, already spent: COMDAT DISCARDS the losing copies, so callers must be re-aimed, which only relocated calls allow. Fix is two parts: a header is recorded IN FLIGHT while its body runs, and any pull requested while one is in flight is QUEUED and run when the outermost completes, at which point nothing is half-emitted. Impls are deliberately not counted as in flight -- their includes sit at the top, so splicing there already IS the top level. 2026-09-02 | frankC | devdocs/dev/debugging-playbook.md | `git log -S` reports commits where the COUNT of a string changed, so it cannot show direction (an addition and a removal are the same hit) and -- the part that actually cost time -- it goes BLIND when a commit conserves the count. Measured on tools/busybox_diff.sh: 9e7c4cf8c removed `-Wl,-z,muldefs` from the link line and in the same diff added a comment saying "the exact failure -Wl,-z,muldefs used to hide", net zero, so `git log -S'muldefs'` does not list it and the only hit is 3056e214c, which ADDED the flag an hour earlier. Read literally the pickaxe says the flag was introduced and never removed, while the file in front of you does not pass it. WHY IT GENERALISES: replacing a use with a comment ABOUT that use is one of the most common commit shapes there is -- a deprecation note, a "why this is gone" line -- so the better a removal is documented, the more likely -S hides it. `git log -G` matches diff content and returns three commits where -S returned one; direction comes from `git show <sha> -- <path> | grep '^[+-].*<str>'` and nowhere else. I asserted the wrong sha into a comment on this basis and caught it only by running git show BEFORE committing rather than after. frankA's framing is the one worth keeping: it is the same animal as the day's other two instrument failures, correct about something adjacent -- occurrence counts, when the question was edits -- and it is the version that survives any amount of care about provenance, because the tool is not quoting anyone.
